            - generic [ref=e180]: Full text access
            - generic [ref=e183]:
              - generic [ref=e184]:
                - heading "Highlights" [level=2] [ref=e185]
                - list [ref=e188]:
                  - listitem [ref=e189]:
                    - generic [ref=e190]: •
                    - generic [ref=e192]: Metabolic modeling redistributed EMP/PPP/ED flux to boost NADPH regeneration.
                  - listitem [ref=e193]:
                    - generic [ref=e194]: •
                    - generic [ref=e196]: Engineered heterologous transhydrogenase system coupled NAD(P)H/ATP co-generation.
                  - listitem [ref=e197]:
                    - generic [ref=e198]: •
                    - generic [ref=e200]: Modified serine-glycine system enhanced 5,10-MTHF driven one‑carbon supply.
                  - listitem [ref=e201]:
                    - generic [ref=e202]: •
                    - generic [ref=e204]: Achieved record D-PA (124.3 g/L, 0.78 g/g glucose) in fed-batch fermentation.
              - generic [ref=e205]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e206]
                - generic [ref=e208]:
                  - text: Pathway reconstitution for high-efficiency chemical production in engineered strains often leads to unbalanced intracellular redox and energy states. To overcome this limitation, we systematically redesigned central metabolism in the engineered strain to enhance redox homeostasis and energy regeneration. This strategy enabled increased production of D-Pantothenic acid (D-PA), an essential vitamin and coenzyme A precursor with applications in feed, pharmaceuticals and other industries. Specifically, genetic modifications targeting NADPH regeneration was conducted. Flux balance analysis (FBA) and flux variability analysis (FVA) were used to predict carbon flux distributions in the Embden-Meyerhof-Parnas (EMP), Pentose Phosphate (PPP), Entner-Doudoroff (ED), and tricarboxylic acid (TCA) pathways. With multi-module coordinated engineering of EMP, PPP and ED pathways, balanced intracellular redox state was established that maximized D-PA production while maintained robust growth, with the D-PA/OD
                  - subscript [ref=e209]: "600"
                  - text: increased from 0.84 to 0.88. By further conducting engineered electron transport chain coupled with overexpressing a heterologous transhydrogenase system from
                  - emphasis [ref=e210]: S. cerevisiae
                  - text: ", intracellular redox state and energy supply was synchronously optimized, and 6.71 g/L D-PA was produced in flask, higher than original 5.65 g/L. Additionally, the 5,10-MTHF pool was optimized"
                  - emphasis [ref=e211]: via
                  - text: optimized serine-glycine system, ensuring sufficient supply of one‑carbon unit for D-PA productions. Integrated implementing of these strategies under a temperature-sensitive switch for decoupling the cell growth and D-PA productions, the achieved strain produced 124.3 g/L D-PA with yield of 0.78 g/g glucose in fed-batch fermentation. These studies demonstrated the importance of maintaining intracellular redox balance and adequate energy supply on high-efficient microbial production of useful chemicals, after the preliminary pathway reconstitutions.
              - generic [ref=e212]:
                - heading "Graphical abstract" [level=2] [ref=e213]
                - figure [ref=e217]:
                  - generic [ref=e218]:
                    - img "Unlabelled Image" [ref=e219]

            - list [ref=e229]:

            - generic [ref=e243]:
              - heading "Keywords" [level=2] [ref=e244]
              - generic [ref=e245]: D-PA;
              - generic [ref=e246]: Sustainable synthesis;
              - generic [ref=e247]: Cofactors;
              - generic [ref=e248]: Metabolic network simulation;
              - generic [ref=e249]: Transhydrogenase cycle;
              - generic [ref=e250]: Redox homeostasis
            - generic [ref=e477]:
              - generic [ref=e478]:
                - generic [ref=e479]:
                  - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e480]
                  - generic [ref=e481]:
                    - text: The efficient production of industrial chemicals has long been a key objective in chemical engineering. Traditionally, this has relied on multi-step chemical synthesis involving using hazardous solvents and energy-intensive conditions [
                    - link "1" [ref=e482] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0005"
                      - generic [ref=e483]: "1"
                    - text: "]. Although such methods have enabled large-scale manufacturing, increasing concerns over environmental sustainability, fossil resource depletion and carbon emissions are driving a shift toward greener, bio-based alternatives ["
                    - link "2" [ref=e484] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0010"
                      - generic [ref=e485]: "2"
                    - text: "]. In this context, the programmable designs of microbial cell factories enabled by synthetic biology, offer transformative alternative to produce compounds from renewable feedstocks under mild, renewable, and sustainable conditions ["
                    - link "3" [ref=e486] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0015"
                      - generic [ref=e487]: "3"
                    - text: "]. Yet realizing this requires careful consideration of enzyme performance, as engineered strain efficiency remains tightly linked to catalytic activities of biosynthetic pathway key enzymes ["
                    - link "4" [ref=e488] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0020"
                      - generic [ref=e489]: "4"
                    - text: "]. Specially, many enzymatic activities are fundamentally dependent on cofactors, which are essential non-protein molecules that facilitate the enzymatic processes required for the synthesis of bio-based chemicals ["
                    - link "5" [ref=e490] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0025"
                      - generic [ref=e491]: "5"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "6" [ref=e492] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0030"
                      - generic [ref=e493]: "6"
                    - text: "]. Among these, nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH), adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate (5,10-MTHF) are especially critical, functioning as the primary cofactors for redox balance, energy provision and C1-donor supply, respectively ["
                    - link "7" [ref=e494] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0035"
                      - generic [ref=e495]: "7"
                    - text: "]. The pathway reconstitutions for high-efficient chemical production in engineered strains often lead to unbalanced overall metabolic homeostasis, by affecting the intracellular availability and dynamic balance of these cofactors ["
                    - link "8" [ref=e496] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0040"
                      - generic [ref=e497]: "8"
                    - text: "]. Consequently, cofactor engineering has become a central strategy in the design of high-efficiency microbial production systems."
                  - generic [ref=e498]:
                    - text: Specifically, cofactor availability mainly affects microbial biosynthesis through cell growth and product formation [
                    - link "9" [ref=e499] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0045"
                      - generic [ref=e500]: "9"
                    - text: "]. On the one hand, cofactors are indispensable for sustaining anabolism and catabolism. NADH and ATP, for instance, are required for cellular macromolecular synthesis, respiration and active transport, directly impacting cell viability and proliferation ["
                    - link "10" [ref=e501] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0050"
                      - generic [ref=e502]: "10"
                    - text: "]. On the other hand, cofactors directly modulate biosynthetic pathways by enabling or limiting the activity of cofactor dependent key enzymes ["
                    - link "11" [ref=e503] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0055"
                      - generic [ref=e504]: "11"
                    - text: "]. Notably, NADPH serves as the reducing agent for various anabolic reactions ["
                    - link "12" [ref=e505] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0060"
                      - generic [ref=e506]: "12"
                    - text: "], whereas 5,10-MTHF supports one‑carbon transfer in the synthesis of amino acids, nucleotides, and vitamins ["
                    - link "13" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0065"
                      - generic [ref=e508]: "13"
                    - text: "]. Meanwhile, given their irreplaceable roles, insufficient cofactor regeneration often leads to redox imbalance, energy deficits and toxic intermediate accumulations, ultimately restricting metabolic flux toward target products ["
                    - link "11" [ref=e509] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0055"
                      - generic [ref=e510]: "11"
                    - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e511]:
                    - text: Therefore, optimizing cofactor utilization is essential to simultaneously increase biomass and maximize the yield of value-added compounds [
                    - link "14" [ref=e512] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0070"
                      - generic [ref=e513]: "14"
                    - text: "]. Most current cofactor engineering strategies focus on enhancing the availability of independent cofactors, such as NADPH or ATP, without considering their interdependence or the broader impact on central metabolism ["
                    - link "15" [ref=e514] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0075"
                      - generic [ref=e515]: "15"
                    - text: "]. In reality, cofactors are regenerated through tightly interconnected pathways, including glycolysis, the pentose phosphate pathway (PPP), the Entner–Doudoroff (ED) pathway and the tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle, which makes their regulation highly complex ["
                    - link "16" [ref=e516] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0080"
                      - generic [ref=e517]: "16"
                    - text: "]. Modifying one branch of metabolism to benefit a particular cofactor may unintentionally compromise another, emphasizing the need for integrated and system-level strategies ["
                    - link "17" [ref=e518] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0085"
                      - generic [ref=e519]: "17"
                    - text: "]. As a result, cofactor optimization must be coupled with fine-tuned metabolic flux redistribution to maintain redox homeostasis, sustain energy supply and avoid metabolic disturbances ["
                    - link "18" [ref=e520] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0090"
                      - generic [ref=e521]: "18"
                    - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e522]:
                    - text: D-PA, a direct precursor of coenzyme A, represents a typical compound to study multi-cofactor dependent metabolic regulation [
                    - link "19" [ref=e523] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0095"
                      - generic [ref=e524]: "19"
                    - text: "]. As a cofactor-intensive dependent pathway, D-PA biosynthesis critically relies on the adequate supply of NADPH, ATP, and 5,10-MTHF ["
                    - link "20" [ref=e525] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0100"
                      - generic [ref=e526]: "20"
                    - text: "]. Previous studies have achieved considerable progress by overexpressing biosynthetic genes or modifying specific cofactor-related enzymes. For example, Zou et al. improved NADPH regeneration by modifying Zwf and Pos5 in plasmids ["
                    - link "21" [ref=e527] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0105"
                      - generic [ref=e528]: "21"
                    - text: "], and further enhanced one‑carbon metabolism through transcriptional regulators CsgD and PurU, achieving a titer of 86.03 g/L in a 5 L fermentor ["
                    - link "22" [ref=e529] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0110"
                      - generic [ref=e530]: "22"
                    - text: "]. Song et al. introduced NAD"
                    - superscript [ref=e531]: +
                    - text: kinase (Ppnk) and an NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e532]: +
                    - text: "-dependent GAPDH (GapCcae), along with"
                    - emphasis [ref=e533]: sthA
                    - text: deletion, to enhance NADPH availability, resulting in a D-PA titer of 83.26 g/L in two-stage fed-batch process [
                    - link "23" [ref=e534] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0115"
                      - generic [ref=e535]: "23"
                    - text: "]. While these studies highlight the importance of cofactor modifications, most have focused on individual elements such as NADPH or one‑carbon metabolism, with limited attempts to systematically integrate cofactor metabolism with carbon flux distributions (Table S1)."
                  - generic [ref=e536]:
                    - text: In our earlier work, intracellular redox balance and ATP supply were not fully optimized, which limited further improvements in yield and productivity [
                    - link "19" [ref=e537] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0095"
                      - generic [ref=e538]: "19"
                    - text: "]. Here, we demonstrated a comprehensive cofactor engineering strategy that simultaneously optimized NADPH, ATP, and one‑carbon metabolism, coupled with dynamic regulation of the TCA cycle, to enhance D-PA production in"
                    - emphasis [ref=e539]: E. coli
                    - text: . Through the screening of endogenous and heterologous genes, NADPH regeneration was firstly enhanced by reprogramming carbon flux and cofactor precursor supply, while limiting unnecessary NADPH consumption. In order to further maintain intracellular NADPH balance, flux balance analysis (FBA) and flux variability analysis (FVA) were applied to regulate the flux through the EMP, PPP, ED, and TCA pathways, improving both redox and metabolic balance. Moreover, a novel heterologous transhydrogenase system from
                    - emphasis [ref=e540]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                    - text: was introduced to convert excess reducing equivalents (surplus NADPH and NADH) into ATP, successfully forming an integrated redox-energy coupling strategy between NAD(
                    - emphasis [ref=e541]: P
                    - text: )H and ATP. In parallel, subunits of the ATP synthase in
                    - emphasis [ref=e542]: E. coli
                    - text: oxidative phosphorylation were fine-tuned, rather than simply overexpressed, to further enhance intracellular ATP levels. In addition to NADPH and ATP pathway optimization, the serine-glycine one‑carbon cycle was engineered to reinforce 5,10-MTHF supply and support the rate-limiting hydroxymethylation steps in D-PA biosynthesis. Finally, strain DPAW10C23 produced high-efficient D-PA in fed-batch fermentation under a temperature-controlled production phase (Table S2), with both titer and yield surpassing previously reported maximums [
                    - link "19" [ref=e543] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0095"
                      - generic [ref=e544]: "19"
                    - text: "]. Overall, this work demonstrates a holistic, multi-module engineering framework focusing on synergistically optimization of cofactor and carbon flux for high-efficiency microbial D-PA production, and offering a feasible strategy for the biosynthesis of other cofactor-dependent chemicals."
                - generic [ref=e545]:
                  - heading "2. Material and methods" [level=2] [ref=e546]
                  - generic [ref=e547]:
                    - heading "2.1. Strains and plasmid construction" [level=3] [ref=e548]
                    - generic [ref=e549]:
                      - generic [ref=e550]:
                        - text: The strains and plasmids employed in this study were summarized in
                        - link "Table 1" [ref=e551] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#t0005"
                          - generic [ref=e552]: Table 1
                        - text: . All engineered strains were constructed using
                        - emphasis [ref=e553]: E. coli
                        - text: W3110 as the parental background, with DPAW10 serving as the starting strain.
                        - emphasis [ref=e554]: E. coli
                        - text: DH5α was employed for routine plasmid propagation and genetic assembly [
                        - link "19" [ref=e555] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0095"
                          - generic [ref=e556]: "19"
                        - text: "]. All primers used were synthesized by Tsingke (Hangzhou, China)."

                    - generic [ref=e931]:
                      - text: Routine molecular biology procedures, including polymerase chain reaction (PCR), agarose gel electrophoresis and bacterial transformation, were performed in accordance with standard protocols [
                      - link "19" [ref=e932] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0095"
                        - generic [ref=e933]: "19"
                      - text: "]. Target gene fragments and linearized plasmid vectors were amplified"
                      - emphasis [ref=e934]: via
                      - text: PCR with sequence-specific primers. Recombinant plasmids were generated using the ClonExpress One-Step Cloning Kit C117 (Vazyme Biotech, Nanjing, China), which ligated the amplified fragments into corresponding vectors.
                    - generic [ref=e935]: Enzymes required for restriction digestion and PCR amplification, including PFU DNA polymerase, were purchased from Vazyme Biotech (Nanjing, China). The integrity of all constructs was confirmed by DNA sequencing by Tsingke (Hangzhou, China).
                  - generic [ref=e936]:
                    - heading "2.2. Genome editing of E. coli" [level=3] [ref=e937]:
                      - text: 2.2. Genome editing of
                      - emphasis [ref=e938]: E. coli
                    - generic [ref=e939]:
                      - text: CRISPR/Cas9-based genome editing was applied to the engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e940]: E. coli
                      - text: strain DPAW10, using the plasmid system pCas and pTarget in accordance with previously established methods [
                      - link "24" [ref=e941] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0120"
                        - generic [ref=e942]: "24"
                      - text: "]. The single-guide RNA (sgRNA), consisting of tracrRNA and crRNA, was designed to include a 20 bp sequence complementary to the intended genomic target. All sgRNA sequences were generated using the CHOPCHOP web server ("
                      - link "https://chopchop.cbu.uib.noOpens in new window" [ref=e943] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://chopchop.cbu.uib.no
                        - generic [ref=e944]:
                          - text: https://chopchop.cbu.uib.no
                          - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e945]
                      - text: ). Guided by the sgRNA, the Cas9 nuclease introduced site-specific double-strand breaks, and homologous recombination was facilitated using 500 bp flanking homology arms for gene integration.
                    - generic [ref=e947]:
                      - text: For transformation,
                      - emphasis [ref=e948]: E. coli
                      - text: competent cells were prepared using the calcium chloride method for pCas introduction, whereas pTarget plasmids were delivered
                      - emphasis [ref=e949]: via
                      - text: electroporation. Recombinant clones were verified by colony PCR, and both editing plasmids were sequentially eliminated following successful genome modification. Each round of editing was verified by PCR and sequencing before the next modification. A full description of the genome engineering workflow is provided in our previous report [
                      - link "22" [ref=e950] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0110"
                        - generic [ref=e951]: "22"
                      - text: "]. And all instances of “Ptrc-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e952]: gene
                      - text: ” in the text indicated that the native promoter was replaced with the trc-promoter and previously deleted loci were used for heterologous expression.
                    - generic [ref=e953]:
                      - text: The respiratory chain related genes were screened using an RNAi-based strategy. Briefly, a 24 bp segment on the P86 plasmid was replaced to serve as the acting site for a custom-designed small RNA (sRNA). This fragment is the reverse 24 bp starting from the start codon of the receptor gene. These synthetic sRNAs are engineered to base-pair with the ribosome-binding region of the target mRNA, thereby blocking ribosome recruitment and inhibiting translation [
                      - link "25" [ref=e954] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0125"
                        - generic [ref=e955]: "25"
                      - text: "]. The mutated P86 plasmid was introduced into engineered strains for shake flask cultivation testing."
                  - generic [ref=e956]:
                    - heading "2.3. Culture conditions" [level=3] [ref=e957]
                    - generic [ref=e958]:
                      - text: All D-pantothenic acid (D-PA) production experiments were conducted in three independent biological replicates, each in a separate shake flask. Single colonies were pre-cultured in 10 mL of Luria-Bertani (LB) broth (10 g/L tryptone, 5 g/L yeast extract, 10 g/L NaCl) at 37 °C with agitation at 200 rpm until the culture reached an optical density (OD
                      - subscript [ref=e959]: "600"
                      - text: ) of 0.6–0.8. Ten percent (
                      - emphasis [ref=e960]: v
                      - text: /v) of this seed culture was then inoculated into 500 mL baffled flasks containing 50 mL of modified Murashige and Skoog (MS) medium. And the culture was subsequently incubated at 30 °C and 180 rpm for 48 h.
                    - generic [ref=e961]:
                      - text: The modified MS formulation consisted of 20 g/L glucose, 16 g/L (NH₄)₂SO₄, 2 g/L yeast extract, 2 g/L KH₂PO₄, 0.5 g/L MgSO₄, plus 1 mL/L of trace metal solution. Prior to inoculation, the medium was supplemented with 2 mg/L vitamin B₁₂, 5 mg/L vitamin B₁, 5 g/L β-alanine, and 0.3 g/L CaCO₃. The trace metal stock comprised 10 g/L CaCl₂, 10 g/L FeSO₄·7H₂O, 1 g/L ZnSO₄·7H₂O, 0.2 g/L CuSO₄, and 0.02 g/L NiCl₂·7H₂O. Enzyme expression was induced at the onset of fermentation by the addition of 0.4 mM IPTG. The intracellular NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e962]: +
                      - text: /NADPH, NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e963]: +
                      - text: /NADH and ATP levels were determined at 12 h of cultivation, which corresponds to the exponential growth phase. Following 48 h of cultivation, cultures were harvested and metabolites were quantified according to standard analytical protocols. And the OD
                      - subscript [ref=e964]: "600"
                      - text: was measured at the same time. This fermentation strategy was systematically optimized to maximize D-PA titer while preserving inter-run consistency in engineered strains [
                      - link "19" [ref=e965] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0095"
                        - generic [ref=e966]: "19"
                      - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e967]:
                    - heading "2.4. Fed-batch fermentation based on DO-feedback strategy" [level=3] [ref=e968]
                    - generic [ref=e969]:
                      - text: In fed-batch fermentation, a 5 L stirred tank bioreactor was employed. A single colony from an LB agar plate was first inoculated into 10 mL of LB broth and grown overnight at 37 °C with shaking at 180 rpm. From this culture, 1 % (
                      - emphasis [ref=e970]: v
                      - text: /v) was transferred into 200 mL of fresh LB in a 500 mL flask and incubated under the same conditions for 8 h to generate the secondary inoculum. The entire secondary seed was then used to inoculate 2 L of YT medium in the bioreactor, which was held at 30 °C throughout the fermentation.
                    - generic [ref=e971]:
                      - text: The YT formulation comprised (per liter) 20 g glucose, 2.5 g ammonium sulfate, 8 g yeast extract, 12 g tryptone, 5 g potassium phosphate (as K₃PO₄), 3 g NaCl, 0.5 g MgSO₄, and 2 g betaine. For fermentation, aeration was maintained at 4 vvm throughout the entire process. The agitation speed started at 300 rpm and was coupled with dissolved oxygen (DO) cascade control. Once DO dropped below 20 %, agitation was raised by 10 rpm increments until it reached 600 rpm, which was maintained until the end of fermentation. The estimated oxygen transfer rate (OTR) is 84.7 mmol O
                      - subscript [ref=e972]: "2"
                      - text: /L/h when DO is controlled at 20 % under 30 °C [
                      - link "26" [ref=e973] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0130"
                        - generic [ref=e974]: "26"
                      - text: "]. The pH was maintained at 6.80 ± 0.05"
                      - emphasis [ref=e975]: via
                      - text: addition of 40 % (
                      - emphasis [ref=e976]: w
                      - text: /w) NH₄OH. During the exponential phase, a vitamin blend (thiamine and cobalamin) and isoleucine, each at the same concentration used in shake-flask assays, were supplemented. A concentrated feed solution (250 g/L glucose, 40 g/L β-alanine) containing identical levels of vitamins and isoleucine was fed intermittently to keep residual glucose between 0 and 5 g/L, thereby supporting sustained biomass accumulation and product formation. Feed solution was supplemented using a DO-stat feeding strategy. Specifically, when the DO level rose above 20 %, the feed solution was automatically added at a flow rate of 0.04 L/h, ensuring sufficient substrate for growth and D-PA production.
                  - generic [ref=e977]:
                    - heading "2.5. Analytical methods" [level=3] [ref=e978]
                    - generic [ref=e979]:
                      - text: Biomass was monitored by measuring OD₆₀₀ on an Ultrospec 3000 spectrophotometer (Pharmacia Biotechnology, Uppsala, Sweden). Consumed glucose was calculated from the volume decrease in feed solution volume indirectly. The total D-PA produced was calculated as the D-PA titer multiplied by the fermentation volume at each sampling point. Residual glucose was quantified with a commercial glucose analyzer according to the method of Liu
                      - emphasis [ref=e980]: et al
                      - text: "["
                      - link "19" [ref=e981] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0095"
                        - generic [ref=e982]: "19"
                      - text: "]. D-PA concentrations were determined"
                      - emphasis [ref=e983]: via
                      - text: high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC; Agilent Technologies, Santa Clara, CA, USA) fitted with a C18 column (5 μm, 250 × 4.6 mm) and an ultraviolet detector set to 200 nm. The maximum yield was defined as the ratio of the peak D-PA concentration to the total glucose fed at that specific time.
                    - generic [ref=e984]: Amino acids (including serine, glycine and cysteine) were analyzed using an LA8080 automatic amino acid analyzer (Hitachi, Tokyo, Japan) with calibration against the manufacturer's H-type amino acid standard mixture. Organic acids were measured by HPLC (Waters 2414 refractive index detector; Waters, Milford, MA, USA) on an Aminex HPX-87H column (300 × 7.8 mm; Bio-Rad, Hercules, CA, USA) at 65 °C with 5 mM H₂SO₄ as the mobile phase.
                    - generic [ref=e985]:
                      - text: Intracellular pyridine nucleotide pools (NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e986]: +
                      - text: /NADPH and NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e987]: +
                      - text: /NADH) were assayed using the WST-8-based quantification kits (Beyotime Biotechnology, Shanghai, China) following the supplier's protocols [
                      - link "27" [ref=e988] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0135"
                        - generic [ref=e989]: "27"
                      - text: "]. NAD("
                      - emphasis [ref=e990]: P
                      - text: )H reduced WST-8 to formazan, and the resulting formazan was quantified to determine the NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e991]: P
                      - text: )H level in the sample. The NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e992]: +
                      - text: concentration was determined by first reducing NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e993]: +
                      - text: to NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e994]: P
                      - text: )H, then measuring the total NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e995]: P
                      - text: )H, and finally subtracting the endogenous NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e996]: P
                      - text: )H amount. Measurements were performed in 96 well plates, with approximately 10
                      - superscript [ref=e997]: "7"
                      - text: cells per well. And NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e998]: P
                      - text: )H levels were determined by comparison to a standard curve provided with the kit, which also gave the unit of “pmol/well”. Adenosine triphosphate (ATP) levels were measured with the enhanced ATP assay kit (Jiancheng Bioengineering Institute, Nanjing, China). The intracellular levels of NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e999]: +
                      - text: /NADPH, NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1000]: +
                      - text: /NADH and ATP were quantified using a microplate reader. And the observed variations in cofactor levels across different strains provided indirect evidence of the effectiveness of the implemented strategies, enabling evaluation of whether a relative cofactor balance was attained.
                    - generic [ref=e1001]:
                      - text: NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1002]: P
                      - text: )H levels were defined as “pmol/well,” equivalent to “pmol/10
                      - superscript [ref=e1003]: "7"
                      - text: cells” in this study. Given that OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1004]: "600"
                      - text: values differed among different engineered strains, normalization per DCW would more accurately reflect intracellular variations. Based on calculations, assuming that 1 OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1005]: "600"
                      - text: corresponded to X cells and Y g DCW in our strains, the NADH level could be calculated as Z pmol/10
                      - superscript [ref=e1006]: "7"
                      - text: cells and NADH per g DCW could also be estimated. Future experiments would be needed to include precise measurement of DCW, enabling a more accurate assessment of NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1007]: P
                      - text: )H levels across different strains and conditions.
                  - generic [ref=e1008]:
                    - heading "2.6. Genome-scale metabolic model prediction" [level=3] [ref=e1009]
                    - generic [ref=e1010]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e1011]: E. coli
                      - text: iML1515 was served as the initial model [
                      - link "28" [ref=e1012] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0140"
                        - generic [ref=e1013]: "28"
                      - text: "] with basic metabolic network shown in Fig. S1A. The glucose and oxygen intake were set at −15 and − 20 mmol/gdw/h, respectively, and biomass growth rate of the strain was identified as the objective function. The FBA ["
                      - link "29" [ref=e1014] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0145"
                        - generic [ref=e1015]: "29"
                      - text: "] and FVA ["
                      - link "30" [ref=e1016] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0150"
                        - generic [ref=e1017]: "30"
                      - text: "] were primarily applied to predict metabolic flux changes after genetic modifications, guiding the engineering of the EMP, PPP, and ED pathways, and were also used to calculate the optimal path, the maximum biomass growth rate, and the maximum and minimum fluxes that each reaction could achieve. The upper and lower bounds of the reactions associated with the target genes were adjusted, thereby enabling the simulation of fluxes through the EMP, ED, and PPP pathways. Meanwhile, gene knockout simulation was realized by setting the bounds of the reaction to 0 and the repression and overexpression simulation were realized by setting the bounds of the reaction to a certain proportion of the flux. Metabolic network flux was obtained by FBA and visualization was realized by Escher ["
                      - link "31" [ref=e1018] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0155"
                        - generic [ref=e1019]: "31"
                      - text: "]. All the analyses were implemented in MATLAB r2022a."
                  - generic [ref=e1020]:
                    - heading "2.7. Statistical analysis" [level=3] [ref=e1021]
                    - generic [ref=e1022]:
                      - text: All assays were conducted in biological triplicate. For shake flask cultivation, the reported values represented the mean of three independent biological replicates, with each replicate measured in a separate shake flask, while for 5 L fermentation, values were obtained from three samplings of a single fermentation. Error bars represented the standard deviation (SD) of three parallel measurements. Statistical significance between groups was evaluated using two-tailed Student's
                      - emphasis [ref=e1023]: t
                      - text: "-tests (for comparisons of two groups) or one-way ANOVA followed by Tukey's"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1024]: post hoc
                      - text: test (for comparisons among three or more groups). Significance levels are indicated as * for
                      - emphasis [ref=e1025]: p
                      - text: < 0.05 and ** for
                      - emphasis [ref=e1026]: p
                      - text: < 0.01. Data plotting and figure assembly were completed using Origin 8.0 (OriginLab Corp., Northampton, MA, USA) and GraphPad Prism 8 (GraphPad Software, San Diego, CA, USA). Some key data about cofactor and flux changes from genetic modifications were listed in Table S3.
                - generic [ref=e1027]:
                  - heading "3. Results" [level=2] [ref=e1028]
                  - generic [ref=e1029]:
                    - heading "3.1. Enhancement of endogenous and heterologous pathways for NADPH regeneration" [level=3] [ref=e1030]
                    - generic [ref=e1031]:
                      - text: The biosynthesis of D-pantothenic acid (D-PA) in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1032]: E. coli
                      - text: is tightly coupled to cofactor availability, particularly NADPH, which is required for the activity of ketol-acid reductoisomerase (IlvC) and ketopantoate reductase (PanE) in the D-PA pathway [
                      - link "27" [ref=e1033] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0135"
                        - generic [ref=e1034]: "27"
                      - text: "]. In addition, NADPH supports the biosynthesis of key precursors such as β-alanine and α-ketoisovalerate, where insufficient supply can constrain overall flux through the pathway ["
                      - link "32" [ref=e1035] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0160"
                        - generic [ref=e1036]: "32"
                      - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e1037]:
                      - generic [ref=e1038]:
                        - text: To enhance NADPH regeneration and improve D-PA production, a modular engineering approach was applied to strain DPAW10, with genes involved in NADPH synthesis and consumption systematically reconfigured (
                        - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1039] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0005"
                          - generic [ref=e1040]: Fig. 1
                        - text: A). Initially, direct NADPH generation was reinforced by overexpressing endogenous PntAB (NADP transhydrogenase) and Pos5 (NADH kinase) from
                        - emphasis [ref=e1041]: S. cerevisiae
                        - text: "["
                        - link "33" [ref=e1042] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0165"
                          - generic [ref=e1043]: "33"
                        - text: "], which elevated intracellular NADPH concentration in strain DPAW10C2 to 48.34 pmol/well (each well containing approximately 10"
                        - superscript [ref=e1044]: "7"
                        - text: cells), nearly 2.0-fold that of the control strain DPAW10 (28.96 pmol/well), with D-PA titer improved from 4.76 g/L to 4.89 g/L (
                        - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1045] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0005"
                          - generic [ref=e1046]: Fig. 1
                        - text: B). It demonstrated that enhancing NADPH availability could increase the D-PA titer of the engineered strain.

                        - paragraph [ref=e1060]:
                          - text: Fig. 1. Enhancing NADPH regeneration through modifications of primary metabolic pathways. The nomenclature ‘
                          - emphasis [ref=e1061]: pntAB
                          - text: ’ in the figure for example, referred to strain DPAW10C1 derived from ‘DPAW10, Ptrc-
                          - emphasis [ref=e1062]: pntAB’
                          - text: and ‘Δ’ represented gene knockout. The superscripts ATG, GTG, and TTG indicated changes in the starting codon.
                    - generic [ref=e1063]:
                      - text: Alternatively, NADPH was indirectly increased by enhancing NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1064]: +
                      - text: phosphorylation to NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1065]: +
                      - emphasis [ref=e1066]: via
                      - text: overexpression of NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1067]: +
                      - text: kinase genes, including
                      - emphasis [ref=e1068]: yfjB
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e1069]: nadK
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1070]: E. coli
                      - text: ) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1071]: ppnK
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1072]: Corynebacterium glutamicum
                      - text: ) [
                      - link "34" [ref=e1073] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0170"
                        - generic [ref=e1074]: "34"
                      - text: "]. Although this strategy increased NADPH levels to 67.97 pmol/well in DPAW10C5, a significant reduction in intracellular NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e1075]: +
                      - text: (23.72 pmol/well) led to impaired growth (OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1076]: "600"
                      - text: from 6.56 in DPAW10C2 to 6.17 in DPAW10C5) (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1077] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1078]: Fig. 1
                      - text: B). Since NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1079]: +
                      - text: is indispensable for the EMP pathway and TCA cycle, its reduced level (from 39.19 pmol/well to 23.72 pmol/well) restricted central carbon metabolism and biomass formation [
                      - link "35" [ref=e1080] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0175"
                        - generic [ref=e1081]: "35"
                      - text: "]. Nevertheless, the increased D-PA production of 5.02 g/L ("
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1082] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1083]: Fig. 1
                      - text: B), could probably be due to pyruvate accumulation (0.33 g/L) under NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1084]: +
                      - text: imbalance (Fig. S2A).
                    - generic [ref=e1085]:
                      - text: To alleviate NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1086]: +
                      - text: depletion,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1087]: nadD
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1088]: nadE
                      - text: ", which encodes nicotinic acid mononucleotide adenylyltransferase and NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e1089]: +
                      - text: synthetase, respectively, were overexpressed [
                      - link "36" [ref=e1090] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0180"
                        - generic [ref=e1091]: "36"
                      - text: "]. Enzyme NadD catalyzes the conversion of NaMN to NaAD, but its activity is reversible and subject to feedback inhibition by NaAD ["
                      - link "37" [ref=e1092] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0185"
                        - generic [ref=e1093]: "37"
                      - text: "], thus the effect of NadD overexpression on NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e1094]: +
                      - text: restoration is limited. In contrast,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1095]: nadE
                      - text: overexpression significantly recovered NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1096]: +
                      - text: concentration from 23.72 pmol/well in DPAW10C5 to 33.45 pmol/well (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1097] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1098]: Fig. 1
                      - text: C), leading to an increased D-PA titer of 5.14 g/L (from 5.02 g/L) with improved cell density (OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1099]: "600"
                      - text: from 6.17 to 6.39) in DPAW10C5–2. Further enhancement was achieved by deleting the NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1100]: +
                      - text: degradation gene
                      - emphasis [ref=e1101]: mazG
                      - text: to reduce NAD dissipation, yielding strain DPAW10C6. This modification restored NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1102]: +
                      - text: to 42.55 pmol/well, further elevated NADPH from 73.28 pmol/well in DPAW10C5–2 to 76.17 pmol/well, while NADH remained nearly unchanged and NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1103]: +
                      - text: slightly decreased. Consequently, D-PA production increased to 5.24 g/L (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1104] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1105]: Fig. 1
                      - text: C), demonstrating that the NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1106]: +
                      - text: limitation was effectively alleviated.
                    - generic [ref=e1107]:
                      - text: Similarly, in order to minimize NADPH dissipation, the soluble transhydrogenase SthA, which converted NADPH to NADH and NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1108]: +
                      - text: "["
                      - link "38" [ref=e1109] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0190"
                        - generic [ref=e1110]: "38"
                      - text: "], was knocked down through start codon weakening and gene deletion strategies. However, all these interventions reduced insufficient NADH (decreasing by 7.37, 14.04, and 38.83 pmol/well in the three strains compared with DPAW10C6, respectively), which in turn led to lower intracellular ATP levels (Fig. S2B), reduced D-PA production, and decreased OD"
                      - subscript [ref=e1111]: "600"
                      - text: "["
                      - link "39" [ref=e1112] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0195"
                        - generic [ref=e1113]: "39"
                      - text: "]. These results might stem from limited NADH availability, which restricted oxidative phosphorylation and overall energy metabolism ["
                      - link "40" [ref=e1114] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0200"
                        - generic [ref=e1115]: "40"
                      - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e1116]:
                      - text: To alleviate the NADH limitation caused by
                      - emphasis [ref=e1117]: sthA
                      - text: knockdown, following previous studies,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1118]: gapC
                      - text: from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1119]: Clostridium acetobutylicum
                      - text: ", encoding an NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1120]: +
                      - text: "-dependent glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase, was introduced to enhance NADPH supply, which could then be converted to NADH"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1121]: via
                      - text: SthA, thereby indirectly relieving NADH depletion [
                      - link "23" [ref=e1122] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0115"
                        - generic [ref=e1123]: "23"
                      - text: "]. It was shown in"
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1124] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1125]: Fig. 1
                      - text: D that the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1126]: sthA
                      - text: variant (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1127]: sthA
                      - superscript [ref=e1128]: GTG
                      - text: ) DPAW10C7–1, combined with
                      - emphasis [ref=e1129]: gapC
                      - text: expression, exhibited increased D-PA production, higher OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1130]: "600"
                      - text: ", and increased NADH and NADPH levels compared with DPAW10C7–2 and DPAW10C7–3. Specifically, at the sampling point, DPAW10C7–1 increased NADPH levels to 78.73 pmol/well and expanded both the (NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e1131]: +
                      - text: + NADH) pool and the (NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1132]: +
                      - text: + NADPH) pool by 3 % each, compared with the control DPAW10C6–1, with OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1133]: "600"
                      - text: improved to 6.33 and D-PA titer improved to 5.34 g/L. Importantly, besides enhancing NADPH supply, expression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1134]: gapC
                      - text: directly redirected glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate to 3-phosphoglycerate, thereby promoting carbon flux toward downstream metabolism [
                      - link "23" [ref=e1135] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0115"
                        - generic [ref=e1136]: "23"
                      - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e1137]:
                      - text: Collectively, these strategies for engineering NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1138]: P
                      - text: )H focused on the complex interdependence between redox cofactors and central metabolism, and further demonstrated that rebalancing these cofactors was important for improving D-PA production and cell growth. Next optimization efforts were tried by fine-tuning glycolysis and TCA cycle fluxes to enhance NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1139]: +
                      - text: /NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1140]: +
                      - text: balance and maximize metabolic efficiency.
                    - generic [ref=e1141]:
                      - text: (A) Metabolic network of different sites modified from DPAW10 to promote cofactor recycling. DPAW10 was engineered to overexpress D-PA biosynthesis genes, enhance precursor supply (pyruvate), and minimize competing pathways (isoleucine) and by-product formation. Dotted lines indicate multiple reaction steps. Genes shown in red represent overexpression, whereas those in green indicate repression. Meanwhile, purple lines represent NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1142]: P
                      - text: )H-dependent reactions, brown lines represent ATP-dependent reactions and blue lines indicate one‑carbon unit-dependent reactions. (B) Changes of D-PA production, cell growth and D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1143]: "600"
                      - text: as well as values of NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1144]: +
                      - text: ", NADH, NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1145]: +
                      - text: and NADPH by engineering NADPH synthesis ways from DPAW10 to DPAW10C5 in shake flask cultivation. (C) Changes of D-PA production, cell growth and D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1146]: "600"
                      - text: as well as values of NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1147]: +
                      - text: ", NADH, NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1148]: +
                      - text: and NADPH by engineering NADH synthesis ways from DPAW10C5 to DPAW10C6 in shake flask cultivation. (D) Changes of D-PA production, cell growth and D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1149]: "600"
                      - text: as well as values of NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1150]: +
                      - text: ", NADH, NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1151]: +
                      - text: and NADPH by engineering
                      - emphasis [ref=e1152]: sthA
                      - text: "-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1153]: gapC
                      - text: pathways from DPAW10C6 to DPAW10C7–3 in shake flask cultivation.
                    - generic [ref=e1154]: GAP, 3-phosphoglyceraldehyde; 3-PG, 3-phosphoglycerate; PEP, phosphoenolpyruvate; ALA, 2-acetolactate; DHIV, 2,3-dihydroxyisovalerate; α-KIV, ketoisovalerate; KPT, keto-pantoate; PT, pantoate; D-PA, D-pantothenic acid; AcCoA, acetyl-CoA; OAA, oxaloacetic acid; FUM, fumaric acid; Asp, aspartate; β-ala, β-alaine; Na, niacin; NM, nicotinamide; NMN, nicotinamide mononucleotide; NaMN, nicotinic acid mononucleotide; NaAD, nicotinate adenine dinucleotide.
                  - generic [ref=e1155]:
                    - heading "3.2. Multi-module switch of carbon metabolism pathway promotes redox homeostasis" [level=3] [ref=e1156]
                    - generic [ref=e1157]:
                      - text: While adjusting NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1158]: P
                      - text: )H levels contributed positively to D-PA biosynthesis, the intrinsic interdependence between cofactor homeostasis, enzymatic activity and central carbon metabolism still presented significant challenges [
                      - link "[21]" [ref=e1159] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0105"
                        - generic [ref=e1160]: "[21]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[22]" [ref=e1161] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0110"
                        - generic [ref=e1162]: "[22]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[23]" [ref=e1163] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0115"
                        - generic [ref=e1164]: "[23]"
                      - text: "]. Redistribution of metabolic flux often perturbs redox balance, resulting in decreased biosynthetic efficiency ["
                      - link "41" [ref=e1165] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0205"
                        - generic [ref=e1166]: "41"
                      - text: "]. To resolve this, we implemented a systematically metabolic engineering strategy guided by FBA and FVA. These tools simulated the reallocation of carbon flux across the EMP, PPP, ED, and TCA pathways to enhance NADPH regeneration without compromising growth, effectively stabilizing redox homeostasis and improving D-PA productivity."
                    - generic [ref=e1167]:
                      - text: In
                      - emphasis [ref=e1168]: E. coli
                      - text: ", glucose metabolism predominantly proceeds"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1169]: via
                      - text: EMP and TCA, while PPP and ED serve as auxiliary pathways. Each pathway contributes differently to energy and redox cofactor generation [
                      - link "42" [ref=e1170] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0210"
                        - generic [ref=e1171]: "42"
                      - text: "]. Specifically, with consumption of 1 mol glucose, EMP generates 2 mol of NADH and 2 mol of ATP. And if only the oxidative PPP is considered, 1 mol of glucose-6-phosphate derived from 1 mol of glucose theoretically produces 2 mol of NADPH, while ED produces 1 mol of NADH, 1 mol of NADPH and 1 mol of ATP. Thus, EMP is thought to mainly support energy and NADH generation. PPP is the main source for NADPH formation"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1172]: via
                      - text: glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (Zwf) and 6-phosphogluconate dehydrogenase (Gnd) while ED provides an alternative route for NADPH formation
                      - emphasis [ref=e1173]: via
                      - text: Zwf [
                      - link "42" [ref=e1174] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0210"
                        - generic [ref=e1175]: "42"
                      - text: "]. To determine the baseline contribution of each pathway, metabolic simulations were conducted and rational engineering strategies were applied subsequently."
                    - generic [ref=e1176]:
                      - generic [ref=e1177]:
                        - text: To begin with, FBA and FVA analyses revealed that blocking PPP and ED
                        - emphasis [ref=e1178]: via zwf
                        - text: deletion channeled 100 % of glycolytic flux into EMP (
                        - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1179] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0010"
                          - generic [ref=e1180]: Fig. 2
                        - text: A, Fig. S1B), leading to the construction of strain DPAW10C8–1. As EMP mainly supplies ATP and NADH but little NADPH, blocking PPP removed the major source for providing NADPH, which restricted NADPH-dependent reactions, thereby disturbing redox balance. This strain exhibited a severe NADPH decline with a slight NADH increase (
                        - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1181] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0010"
                          - generic [ref=e1182]: Fig. 2
                        - text: B). Meanwhile, the resulting NADPH depletion disturbed redox balance, limited biomass accumulation and D-PA production, confirming the essential role of PPP in maintaining intracellular redox homeostasis. In addition, FBA prediction revealed that although all PEP was assumed to be converted into pyruvate under the
                        - emphasis [ref=e1183]: zwf
                        - text: deletion condition, yet only 89 % of pyruvate flux was directed to acetyl-CoA and subsequently entered the TCA (Fig. S1B), suggesting that the weakened TCA flux might have led to the reduced biomass.
                      - figure [ref=e1184]:
                        - generic [ref=e1185]:
                          - img "Fig. 2" [ref=e1186]
                          - list [ref=e1187]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1188]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (1MB)" [ref=e1189] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S1385894725100223-gr2_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1191]: "Download: Download high-res image (1MB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1192]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1193] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S1385894725100223-gr2.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1195]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1198]:
                          - text: Fig. 2. Analyzing the effects of modifications on various pathways of glycolysis through computer simulation of metabolic flux. The nomenclature ‘
                          - emphasis [ref=e1199]: Ppzwf
                          - text: +
                          - emphasis [ref=e1200]: Pppgl
                          - emphasis [ref=e1201]: +
                          - emphasis [ref=e1202]: Ppedd
                          - emphasis [ref=e1203]: +
                          - emphasis [ref=e1204]: Ppeda
                          - text: ’ in the figure for example, referred to strain DPAW10C9 derived from ‘DPAW10C8-3 derivative, Ptrc-
                          - emphasis [ref=e1205]: Ppzwf
                          - text: /Ptrc-
                          - emphasis [ref=e1206]: Pppgl
                          - text: /Ptrc-
                          - emphasis [ref=e1207]: Ppedd
                          - text: /Ptrc-
                          - emphasis [ref=e1208]: Ppeda’
                          - text: and ‘Δ’ represented gene knockout. The superscripts ATG, GTG, and TTG indicated changes in the starting codon. Negative flux values indicate that the reaction runs opposite to the direction of the arrows.
                    - generic [ref=e1209]:
                      - text: Besides, after simulations, PPP-exclusive metabolism was assessed by deleting
                      - emphasis [ref=e1210]: pgi
                      - text: (phosphoglucose isomerase in EMP) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1211]: edd
                      - text: (6-phosphogluconate dehydratase in ED) to divert flux from EMP and block ED, respectively, forcing glucose catabolism entirely through PPP (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1212] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1213]: Fig. 2
                      - text: B, Fig. S1C). This attempt extremely increased NADPH levels to 85.11 pmol/well but depleted NADH (nearly 20 % less than that of the control) (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1214] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1215]: Fig. 2
                      - text: B). Excessive NADPH depleted the NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1216]: +
                      - text: pool and elevated the NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1217]: +
                      - text: ratio, thereby limiting NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1218]: +
                      - text: "-dependent pathways and associated metabolic process, such as the reaction guided by GapCcae, and restricting cell growth. On the other hand, FBA under this attempt revealed a reduction in the flux from PEP to pyruvate to 94.9 %, along with a decrease in the flux from pyruvate to acetyl-CoA to 28.8 % (Fig. S1C), which also indirectly explained the observed decline in OD"
                      - subscript [ref=e1219]: "600"
                      - text: (4.63). Although the D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1220]: "600"
                      - text: ratio of DPAW10C8–2 improved to 0.82, D-PA yield plunged due to growth inhibition and metabolic imbalance (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1221] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1222]: Fig. 2
                      - text: B).
                    - generic [ref=e1223]:
                      - text: Similarly, to evaluate the isolate function of the ED pathway, simulations were performed for the knockouts of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1224]: pgi
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1225]: gnd
                      - text: ", and subsequently these genes were deleted to force flux through ED (Fig. S1D). This routing theoretically improved cofactor yield efficiency due to the shorter pathways without carbon loss (from 6PG to Ru5P), but in practice, DPAW10C8–3 still showed deficiency in OD"
                      - subscript [ref=e1226]: "600"
                      - text: ", D-PA production, NADH and NADPH levels ("
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1227] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1228]: Fig. 2
                      - text: B), suggesting insufficient flux through ED pathway as the activities of its native key enzymes remained consistently low [
                      - link "43" [ref=e1229] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0215"
                        - generic [ref=e1230]: "43"
                      - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e1231]:
                      - text: Despite its inefficiency in the native ED pathway, DPAW10C9 achieved the highest D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1232]: "600"
                      - text: of 0.88, demonstrating ED's potential. To activate this pathway more effectively, the ED pathway from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1233]: Pseudomonas putida
                      - text: ", known for higher enzyme activities and more effective NADPH supply, was introduced by replacing genes"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1234]: zwf
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e1235]: pgl
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e1236]: eda
                      - text: ", and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1237]: edd
                      - text: "["
                      - link "43" [ref=e1238] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0215"
                        - generic [ref=e1239]: "43"
                      - text: "]. Although a slight decrease in NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1240]: +
                      - text: was observed, such engineering strategy significantly improved NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1241]: P
                      - text: )H levels, raised the D-PA titer by 0.8 g/L compared with DPAW10C8–3 (4.19 g/L), and improved cell growth, with OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1242]: "600"
                      - text: rising from 4.83 to 5.57 (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1243] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1244]: Fig. 2
                      - text: C). However, EMP and PPP disruption still limited cell growth and D-PA production, making the overall performance of DPAW10C9 inferior to that of DPAW10C7–1 and indicating the need for further metabolic flux optimization.
                    - generic [ref=e1245]:
                      - text: To address the metabolic issue caused by EMP and PPP disruption,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1246]: pgi
                      - text: was reintroduced in DPAW10C9 (Fig. S1E), which reactivated EMP and promoted cell growth (OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1247]: "600"
                      - text: of 6.67)
                      - emphasis [ref=e1248]: via
                      - text: enhanced glucose catabolism. However, this shifted excessive flux into EMP, leading to decreased D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1249]: "600"
                      - text: (0.74). This could be due to that reactivation of EMP increased ATP and NADH supply to support growth, but diverted flux away from ED in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1250]: E. coli
                      - text: "["
                      - link "44" [ref=e1251] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0220"
                        - generic [ref=e1252]: "44"
                      - text: "], thereby reducing NADPH availability for D-PA biosynthesis and lowering the D-PA/OD"
                      - subscript [ref=e1253]: "600"
                      - text: ratio. To mitigate this, after FBA simulation, a hypomorphic
                      - emphasis [ref=e1254]: pgi
                      - text: mutant (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1255]: pgi
                      - superscript [ref=e1256]: H386Y
                      - text: ) was introduced (Fig. S1F) to weaken Pgi activity and reduced EMP flux [
                      - link "45" [ref=e1257] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0225"
                        - generic [ref=e1258]: "45"
                      - text: "]. According to FBA and FVA simulations, carbon flux in DPAW10C10 was redistributed with 36 % directed to the ED pathway and 64 % to the EMP pathway ("
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1259] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e1260]: Fig. 1
                      - text: F), which finally achieved improved cell growth (OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1261]: "600"
                      - text: of 6.48) and a D-PA titer of 5.58 g/L without evident NADPH deficiency (73.51 pmol/well). At the same time, FBA analysis indicated that the flux from pyruvate to acetyl-CoA recovered to 94.2 %, validating that targeted reallocation of metabolic flux supported robust growth and redox homeostasis.
                    - generic [ref=e1262]:
                      - text: Further refinement of the PPP was carried out to support essential intracellular biosynthesis and cofactor generation. Since a minimal PPP flux (5–10 %) was reported to be required for ribose-5-phosphate (R5P), NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1263]: +
                      - text: ", nucleotide and vitamin synthesis ["
                      - link "46" [ref=e1264] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0230"
                        - generic [ref=e1265]: "46"
                      - text: "],"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1266]: gnd
                      - text: expression was fine-tuned
                      - emphasis [ref=e1267]: via
                      - text: start codon engineering and its effects on cell growth and production performance were evaluated (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1268] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1269]: Fig. 2
                      - text: A, Fig. S1G). It could be seen in
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1270] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1271]: Fig. 2
                      - text: C that DPAW10C10–3, carrying the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1272]: gnd
                      - superscript [ref=e1273]: TTG
                      - text: variant, achieved the best performance with 10 % PPP flux (estimated by FBA simulation). Strain DPAW10C10–3 likely maintained the supply of R5P and NADPH for D-PA biosynthesis, without causing NADPH over-accumulation or NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1274]: +
                      - text: depletion, and thereby contained optimized NAD(
                      - emphasis [ref=e1275]: P
                      - text: )H/NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e1276]: +
                      - text: pools for achieving high-efficient D-PA production of 5.65 g/L and the best D-PA/OD
                      - subscript [ref=e1277]: "600"
                      - text: (0.88) as well (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1278] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e1279]: Fig. 2
                      - text: C). Additionally, although the PEP-to-pyruvate flux remained at 95 %, the flux from pyruvate to acetyl-CoA reached 99.3 %, indirectly demonstrating that the rational introduction of PPP enhanced the TCA cycle (Fig. S1G).
                    - generic [ref=e1280]: Overall, integrating FBA/FVA predictions with experimental data enabled the identification of key metabolic nodes, while also helped guiding pathway modifications and explaining observed changes in biomass and metabolites through flux variations. Based on this, the multi-module coordinated engineering of EMP, PPP, and ED pathways established a balanced redox state that maximized D-PA production while maintaining cellular growth. However, as FBA and FVA cannot fully replace experimental validation, future isotope labeling experiments (not performed in this study) will be necessary to carry out to confirm the agreement between model predictions and actual results.
                    - generic [ref=e1281]:

(B) Changes of D-PA production, cell growth and D-PA/OD
